Lately it feels more like something I have to drag myself toward.\n\nSo… yes, less energy, but I guess I’d describe it more as less drive or less spark than just being physically tired. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think I’d say “worthless,” no. That word feels… stronger than what I experience. I don’t walk around thinking I’m a bad person or that I don’t have value.

I do blame myself for things sometimes, but mostly in a practical way, if that makes sense. Like if I miss a detail or something takes longer than it should, I’ll replay it and think, “Why didn’t I catch that?” or “I should have handled that better.” But it doesn’t usually turn into this deep feeling that I’m worthless. It’s more frustration with myself, or being annoyed that I’m not performing the way I expect myself to.

So… I can be hard on myself, definitely. But I don’t think I have that constant guilt or worthlessness thing you’re asking about.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F32.2, F32.3\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I came in because I’ve noticed I’m just not really doing the things that normally make me feel like me. Like, I usually go on these photography walks — nothing fancy, just getting out and noticing light and buildings and people, that kind of thing — and lately even the thought of picking up the camera feels heavy. Flat, I guess.\n\nAnd I mentor kids too, or I have been, and that used to feel meaningful, like it gave me a sense of being useful and connected. But I’ve stopped initiating that. I don’t reach out the way I normally would. Even at work, keeping a clean calendar is sort of my thing — I’m an executive assistant, so organization is usually where I feel competent — but now even that feels like pushing a boulder uphill.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t know these things matter to me. I do. They just don’t seem to spark anything, and I’m not starting them on my own. That’s what made me think I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy the activities you're doing, even if you're not initiating them as much as you used to?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way, no. I mean, if I’m already in the middle of something, sometimes I can recognize, like, “Okay, this used to be something I cared about,” but it doesn’t really land emotionally the way it used to.\n\nWith the photography walks, for example, if I force myself to go out or I happen to have my camera with me, I can still notice things — light on a building, somebody’s shadow, little details like that — but it feels more like I’m checking the boxes of being the person who does that, rather than actually enjoying it. It’s kind of flat. Heavy, even before I start.\n\nAnd mentoring the kids… that one bothers me because it used to feel meaningful. Like, I’d leave feeling connected and useful. Now I don’t initiate it much, and even when I think about reaching out, there’s this dullness to it. Same with my calendar and work organization — normally that gives me a sense of control or competence, but lately it just feels like pushing myself through motions. So, yeah, I can still do some of it if it’s in front of me, but the enjoyment and the spark are pretty muted.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad most of the day, exactly. That word feels… I’m not sure. It’s more like flat or weighed down, like I was saying. Sometimes I’ll catch myself thinking, “I should feel something about this,” but I’m not necessarily sitting there crying or feeling actively sad all day.\n\nThere are moments where I feel low, sure, but if you asked me to track it hour by hour, I don’t know that I could honestly say, yes, sad most of the day. It’s more muted than sad, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find pleasure in anything at all lately?\n\nPatient: A little, maybe, but it’s pretty muted. That’s part of what scared me enough to come in. The things that usually make me feel like myself — going out for photography walks, mentoring the kids, even keeping my work calendar really clean and organized — they’ve all started feeling flat and heavy.\n\nIt’s not like I hate them now. If I’m already doing something, I can sometimes notice that it should feel good, or that it used to feel good. Like with photography, I’ll still see a nice angle or the light hitting a building and think, “That’s interesting.” But it doesn’t really turn into pleasure. It’s more like an observation than enjoyment.\n\nAnd the bigger thing is I’m not starting things on my own. I used to naturally reach for the camera or check in about mentoring, and now there’s this heaviness before I even begin. So… pleasure isn’t completely gone, I guess, but it feels very dulled down, and I’m not seeking things out the way I normally would.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I haven’t been tracking it closely. My clothes don’t feel dramatically different or anything that I’ve noticed, and I haven’t been weighing myself regularly.\n\nI mean, maybe there are normal little fluctuations, but nothing where I’d say, “Oh, I’ve clearly lost weight,” or “I’ve gained a lot.” If you need an exact number, I’d probably have to check, because right now I’d just be guessing.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost any weight unintentionally?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve noticed, no. I mean, I haven’t stepped on a scale and tracked it in any careful way, so I don’t want to say absolutely not if I don’t actually know. But there hasn’t been anything obvious, like my pants suddenly being loose or people commenting on it or me thinking, “Wow, I’m thinner.”\n\nSo if there has been any change, it hasn’t been noticeable to me. I wouldn’t say I’ve unintentionally lost weight, at least not in a way I can point to.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still look forward to or feel excited about?\n\nPatient: Not really excited, no. I keep trying to think of something because I don’t want to sound like everything is completely gone, but… the things I normally would look forward to just don’t pull me in the same way.\n\nLike photography walks used to be a real thing for me — I’d notice the weather or the light and think, “I should take the camera out.” Or with mentoring the kids, I used to feel some purpose around that, like it mattered and I was glad to show up. Even something boring like keeping my work calendar clean and organized used to make me feel settled, like I was on top of things. And lately all of that feels flat and heavy.\n\nThe part that worries me is I’m not really initiating any of it. If something is already in front of me, I can sort of go through the motions, but I’m not looking forward to it ahead of time. I’m not getting that little spark of, “Oh, I want to do this.” That’s actually a big reason I came in — I stopped reaching for the things that usually make me feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still enjoy or find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Enjoy is probably too strong a word. I can still do some of the activities, but I don’t really get pleasure from them the way I used to.\n\nLike, with photography walks, I might think about it in this distant way, like, “That’s something I used to like,” but I don’t feel that pull to actually grab the camera and go out. Mentoring the kids is similar — I know it matters, and I know it used to make me feel useful and connected, but lately it feels more like I’m just showing up because I’m supposed to, not because I’m getting anything from it emotionally.\n\nEven something small like keeping my calendar clean — which sounds silly, but it used to give me this little sense of order, like I had things under control — now it just feels flat and heavy. That’s really what made me come in, honestly. The things that usually make me feel like myself stopped feeling rewarding, and then I noticed I wasn’t initiating them anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure from activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s fair to say. I don’t know if it’s completely unable, like I can still recognize that something is “supposed” to be enjoyable, if that makes sense. But the actual feeling of pleasure just isn’t really there.\n\nWhat made me come in is that the things that usually make me feel like myself — photography walks, mentoring the kids, even something as small as keeping a clean calendar — started feeling flat and heavy. And then I noticed I just stopped initiating them. I wasn’t thinking, “Oh, I want to go take pictures,” or “I’m looking forward to seeing the kids,” or even getting that little satisfying feeling from being organized. It’s more like I know those things used to matter to me, but I’m kind of standing outside of them now.\n\nSo yes, I guess I’d say I’m not really experiencing pleasure from them the way I used to. They’re still there, but the reward part feels muted or missing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things you've done or haven't done?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say “often,” exactly. I mean, I can feel bad about things, sure — like if I don’t follow through on something or I feel like I wasn’t as present as I should’ve been. But I’m not walking around with this constant, crushing guilt about something specific I did.\n\nIt’s more vague than that, if anything. Like I’ll think, “I should be doing better,” or “I’m letting things slide,” but I don’t know if that’s guilt or just frustration with myself. I don’t have one big thing I’m blaming myself for. It’s more… I notice I’m not doing what I normally do, and then I feel kind of disappointed in myself, but I’m not sure I’d call it guilt in the strong sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant weight loss over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve really noticed, no. I mean, my clothes don’t feel dramatically different or anything, and I haven’t had one of those moments where I step on a scale and think, “Oh wow, that’s a big drop.”\n\nI also haven’t been tracking it very closely, so I don’t want to sound overly certain. My weight can move around a little depending on routine and how busy I am, but as far as significant weight loss over the past few months… I don’t think so. Nothing that stood out to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving more slowly than usual or having trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah… more the trouble sitting still side, I think. Not so much that I’m physically pacing every second, but during those stretches I get this feeling like if I stop moving from one thing to the next, something important is going to fall through the cracks.\n\nI’ll start rebuilding work templates, then I’ll jump into making care plans, then I’m emailing relatives, then reorganizing files — and it’s all kind of happening at once. It doesn’t feel random to me in the moment; it feels like I’m being productive and responsible, like I’m finally catching everything. But looking at it after, it’s a lot. I’m bouncing between tasks, opening folders, making lists, revising systems that probably didn’t need revising right then.\n\nSo yes, sitting still can feel uncomfortable, but not just in a restless-leg kind of way. It’s more like sitting still means I’m dropping the ball, or being careless, and I need to keep doing something to stay ahead of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving more slowly than you used to?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I wouldn’t say I feel slowed down in that way. Like, I don’t feel like I’m dragging my body through the day or that it takes me forever to get from one room to another.\n\nIf anything, when I notice myself being “slower,” it’s usually more because I’m overthinking what I’m doing — like rereading something, checking if I did it correctly, trying to decide the right order to do things in. But physically moving more slowly than I used to? I haven’t really noticed that. At least not enough that I’d think, “Oh, something’s changed there.”\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are moving more slowly than they used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the sense that my thoughts feel slow or sluggish. I wouldn’t describe it like my mind is stuck in mud or like I’m having long blank pauses where I can’t think of what comes next.\n\nSometimes I do take longer to answer something, but that’s usually because I’m trying to sort out the exact wording or I’m checking myself before I say it. Like, I’ll think, “Is that accurate? Am I overstating it? Did I miss part of the question?” So it can look like I’m slow, maybe, but internally it doesn’t feel like the thoughts are moving slowly. It feels more like I’m trying to organize them properly.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed my thoughts slowing down compared to how I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like it takes you longer than usual to complete daily tasks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… some things do take longer, but it’s not like I can’t do them. My work is still mostly intact — I’m still getting things done, people aren’t having to take over for me or anything like that. But I’ve noticed I’m not as automatic as I used to be.\n\nLike calendar stuff used to be very second nature for me. I could hold a lot of moving pieces in my head and catch little details almost without thinking. Lately I’ve missed small calendar details, or I’ll catch myself going back over something two or three times because I don’t quite trust that I saw it correctly the first time. So a task that should be quick — checking a meeting time, confirming who’s included, making sure the attachment is right — can stretch out because I’m double-checking things that used to feel routine.\n\nAnd outside of work, I guess I’ve pulled back some. Photography outings, mentoring — things I used to show up for more easily. It’s not that I don’t care about them. I do. But I don’t feel as present or as reliable, so I hesitate, and then I end up not going or not committing. So daily tasks, yes, some take longer, but it’s more that I’m second-guessing and trying not to miss something than moving slowly physically.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have less energy than you used to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would say I have less energy, but it’s not exactly like I’m exhausted all the time or falling asleep at my desk. It’s more like the usual “push” isn’t there. The things that normally make me feel like me — photography walks, mentoring the kids, even just staying on top of a clean calendar — they’ve started to feel kind of flat and heavy.\n\nLike with photography, I used to see a free afternoon and think, “Oh, I should go out, maybe catch the light somewhere,” and it would feel natural. Now I’ll think about it, and then there’s this weight to it, like getting myself to initiate it feels bigger than it should. Same with mentoring — I care about it, I really do, but starting the process of showing up or reaching out feels harder. And even at work, the calendar used to almost give me energy in a weird way, like keeping it clean made me feel competent and on top of things. Lately it feels more like something I have to drag myself toward.\n\nSo… yes, less energy, but I guess I’d describe it more as less drive or less spark than just being physically tired. That’s actually a big part of why I came in — I noticed I’d stopped initiating the things that usually keep me connected to myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel worthless or blame yourself for things?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say “worthless,” no. That word feels… stronger than what I experience. I don’t walk around thinking I’m a bad person or that I don’t have value.\n\nI do blame myself for things sometimes, but mostly in a practical way, if that makes sense. Like if I miss a detail or something takes longer than it should, I’ll replay it and think, “Why didn’t I catch that?” or “I should have handled that better.” But it doesn’t usually turn into this deep feeling that I’m worthless. It’s more frustration with myself, or being annoyed that I’m not performing the way I expect myself to.\n\nSo… I can be hard on myself, definitely. But I don’t think I have that constant guilt or worthlessness thing you’re asking about.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I wouldn’t say I feel slowed down in that way. Like, I don’t feel like I’m dragging my body through the day or that it takes me forever to get from one room to another.\n\nIf anything, when I notice myself being “slower,” it’s usually more because I’m overthinking what I’m doing — like rereading something, checking if I did it correctly, trying to decide the right order to do things in. But physically moving more slowly than I used to? I haven’t really noticed that. At least not enough that I’d think, “Oh, something’s changed there.”\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are moving more slowly than they used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the sense that my thoughts feel slow or sluggish. I wouldn’t describe it like my mind is stuck in mud or like I’m having long blank pauses where I can’t think of what comes next.\n\nSometimes I do take longer to answer something, but that’s usually because I’m trying to sort out the exact wording or I’m checking myself before I say it. Like, I’ll think, “Is that accurate? Am I overstating it? Did I miss part of the question?” So it can look like I’m slow, maybe, but internally it doesn’t feel like the thoughts are moving slowly. It feels more like I’m trying to organize them properly.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed my thoughts slowing down compared to how I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like it takes you longer than usual to complete daily tasks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… some things do take longer, but it’s not like I can’t do them. My work is still mostly intact — I’m still getting things done, people aren’t having to take over for me or anything like that. But I’ve noticed I’m not as automatic as I used to be.\n\nLike calendar stuff used to be very second nature for me. I could hold a lot of moving pieces in my head and catch little details almost without thinking. Lately I’ve missed small calendar details, or I’ll catch myself going back over something two or three times because I don’t quite trust that I saw it correctly the first time. So a task that should be quick — checking a meeting time, confirming who’s included, making sure the attachment is right — can stretch out because I’m double-checking things that used to feel routine.\n\nAnd outside of work, I guess I’ve pulled back some. Photography outings, mentoring — things I used to show up for more easily. It’s not that I don’t care about them. I do. But I don’t feel as present or as reliable, so I hesitate, and then I end up not going or not committing. So daily tasks, yes, some take longer, but it’s more that I’m second-guessing and trying not to miss something than moving slowly physically.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have less energy than you used to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would say I have less energy, but it’s not exactly like I’m exhausted all the time or falling asleep at my desk. It’s more like the usual “push” isn’t there. The things that normally make me feel like me — photography walks, mentoring the kids, even just staying on top of a clean calendar — they’ve started to feel kind of flat and heavy.\n\nLike with photography, I used to see a free afternoon and think, “Oh, I should go out, maybe catch the light somewhere,” and it would feel natural. Now I’ll think about it, and then there’s this weight to it, like getting myself to initiate it feels bigger than it should. Same with mentoring — I care about it, I really do, but starting the process of showing up or reaching out feels harder. And even at work, the calendar used to almost give me energy in a weird way, like keeping it clean made me feel competent and on top of things. Lately it feels more like something I have to drag myself toward.\n\nSo… yes, less energy, but I guess I’d describe it more as less drive or less spark than just being physically tired. That’s actually a big part of why I came in — I noticed I’d stopped initiating the things that usually keep me connected to myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel worthless or blame yourself for things?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say “worthless,” no. That word feels… stronger than what I experience. I don’t walk around thinking I’m a bad person or that I don’t have value.\n\nI do blame myself for things sometimes, but mostly in a practical way, if that makes sense. I don’t think so… not in the sense of my body actually moving slowly. Like, I’m not shuffling around or feeling like my arms and legs are heavy or delayed or anything like that.

If someone watched me walk down the hall or make coffee or get up from my desk, I don’t think they’d say I’m moving slower than usual. It’s more that I can get stuck before I start something, or I’ll pause and think through a step longer than I used to. But physically, once I’m up and doing it, I don’t really notice my body being slower.

So… no, I haven’t really noticed that. It feels more mental than physical, I guess.

That’s really what made me come in, honestly. The things that usually make me feel like myself stopped feeling rewarding, and then I noticed I wasn’t initiating them anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure from activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s fair to say. I don’t know if it’s completely unable, like I can still recognize that something is “supposed” to be enjoyable, if that makes sense. But the actual feeling of pleasure just isn’t really there.\n\nWhat made me come in is that the things that usually make me feel like myself — photography walks, mentoring the kids, even something as small as keeping a clean calendar — started feeling flat and heavy. And then I noticed I just stopped initiating them. I wasn’t thinking, “Oh, I want to go take pictures,” or “I’m looking forward to seeing the kids,” or even getting that little satisfying feeling from being organized. It’s more like I know those things used to matter to me, but I’m kind of standing outside of them now.\n\nSo yes, I guess I’d say I’m not really experiencing pleasure from them the way I used to. They’re still there, but the reward part feels muted or missing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things you've done or haven't done?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say “often,” exactly. I mean, I can feel bad about things, sure — like if I don’t follow through on something or I feel like I wasn’t as present as I should’ve been. But I’m not walking around with this constant, crushing guilt about something specific I did.\n\nIt’s more vague than that, if anything. Like I’ll think, “I should be doing better,” or “I’m letting things slide,” but I don’t know if that’s guilt or just frustration with myself. I don’t have one big thing I’m blaming myself for. It’s more… I notice I’m not doing what I normally do, and then I feel kind of disappointed in myself, but I’m not sure I’d call it guilt in the strong sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant weight loss over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve really noticed, no. I mean, my clothes don’t feel dramatically different or anything, and I haven’t had one of those moments where I step on a scale and think, “Oh wow, that’s a big drop.”\n\nI also haven’t been tracking it very closely, so I don’t want to sound overly certain. My weight can move around a little depending on routine and how busy I am, but as far as significant weight loss over the past few months… I don’t think so. Nothing that stood out to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving more slowly than usual or having trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah… more the trouble sitting still side, I think. Not so much that I’m physically pacing every second, but during those stretches I get this feeling like if I stop moving from one thing to the next, something important is going to fall through the cracks.\n\nI’ll start rebuilding work templates, then I’ll jump into making care plans, then I’m emailing relatives, then reorganizing files — and it’s all kind of happening at once. It doesn’t feel random to me in the moment; it feels like I’m being productive and responsible, like I’m finally catching everything. But looking at it after, it’s a lot. I’m bouncing between tasks, opening folders, making lists, revising systems that probably didn’t need revising right then.\n\nSo yes, sitting still can feel uncomfortable, but not just in a restless-leg kind of way. It’s more like sitting still means I’m dropping the ball, or being careless, and I need to keep doing something to stay ahead of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving more slowly than you used to?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I wouldn’t say I feel slowed down in that way. Like, I don’t feel like I’m dragging my body through the day or that it takes me forever to get from one room to another.\n\nIf anything, when I notice myself being “slower,” it’s usually more because I’m overthinking what I’m doing — like rereading something, checking if I did it correctly, trying to decide the right order to do things in. But physically moving more slowly than I used to? I haven’t really noticed that. At least not enough that I’d think, “Oh, something’s changed there.”\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are moving more slowly than they used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the sense that my thoughts feel slow or sluggish. I wouldn’t describe it like my mind is stuck in mud or like I’m having long blank pauses where I can’t think of what comes next.\n\nSometimes I do take longer to answer something, but that’s usually because I’m trying to sort out the exact wording or I’m checking myself before I say it. Like, I’ll think, “Is that accurate? Am I overstating it? Did I miss part of the question?” So it can look like I’m slow, maybe, but internally it doesn’t feel like the thoughts are moving slowly. It feels more like I’m trying to organize them properly.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed my thoughts slowing down compared to how I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like it takes you longer than usual to complete daily tasks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… some things do take longer, but it’s not like I can’t do them. My work is still mostly intact — I’m still getting things done, people aren’t having to take over for me or anything like that. But I’ve noticed I’m not as automatic as I used to be.\n\nLike calendar stuff used to be very second nature for me. I could hold a lot of moving pieces in my head and catch little details almost without thinking. Lately I’ve missed small calendar details, or I’ll catch myself going back over something two or three times because I don’t quite trust that I saw it correctly the first time. So a task that should be quick — checking a meeting time, confirming who’s included, making sure the attachment is right — can stretch out because I’m double-checking things that used to feel routine.\n\nAnd outside of work, I guess I’ve pulled back some. Photography outings, mentoring — things I used to show up for more easily. It’s not that I don’t care about them. I do. But I don’t feel as present or as reliable, so I hesitate, and then I end up not going or not committing. So daily tasks, yes, some take longer, but it’s more that I’m second-guessing and trying not to miss something than moving slowly physically.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have less energy than you used to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would say I have less energy, but it’s not exactly like I’m exhausted all the time or falling asleep at my desk. It’s more like the usual “push” isn’t there. The things that normally make me feel like me — photography walks, mentoring the kids, even just staying on top of a clean calendar — they’ve started to feel kind of flat and heavy.\n\nLike with photography, I used to see a free afternoon and think, “Oh, I should go out, maybe catch the light somewhere,” and it would feel natural. Now I’ll think about it, and then there’s this weight to it, like getting myself to initiate it feels bigger than it should. Same with mentoring — I care about it, I really do, but starting the process of showing up or reaching out feels harder. And even at work, the calendar used to almost give me energy in a weird way, like keeping it clean made me feel competent and on top of things. Lately it feels more like something I have to drag myself toward.\n\nSo… yes, less energy, but I guess I’d describe it more as less drive or less spark than just being physically tired. That’s actually a big part of why I came in — I noticed I’d stopped initiating the things that usually keep me connected to myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel worthless or blame yourself for things?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say “worthless,” no. That word feels… stronger than what I experience. I don’t walk around thinking I’m a bad person or that I don’t have value.\n\nI do blame myself for things sometimes, but mostly in a practical way, if that makes sense. Like if I miss a detail or something takes longer than it should, I’ll replay it and think, “Why didn’t I catch that?” or “I should have handled that better.” But it doesn’t usually turn into this deep feeling that I’m worthless. It’s more frustration with myself, or being annoyed that I’m not performing the way I expect myself to.\n\nSo… I can be hard on myself, definitely. But I don’t think I have that constant guilt or worthlessness thing you’re asking about.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your body is moving more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the sense of my body actually moving slowly. Like, I’m not shuffling around or feeling like my arms and legs are heavy or delayed or anything like that.\n\nIf someone watched me walk down the hall or make coffee or get up from my desk, I don’t think they’d say I’m moving slower than usual. It’s more that I can get stuck before I start something, or I’ll pause and think through a step longer than I used to. But physically, once I’m up and doing it, I don’t really notice my body being slower.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed that. It feels more mental than physical, I guess.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I wouldn’t say “almost all” in this absolute way, but… yeah, the pleasure is definitely dulled. That’s actually one of the main reasons I came in.

The things that usually make me feel like myself started feeling flat and heavy. Like photography walks — I used to look forward to taking my camera out, noticing light, finding small things to frame. It wasn’t some huge event, it was just something that gave me a little spark. Lately I’ll think about doing it, and instead of feeling interested, it feels like a chore before I’ve even started.

Same with mentoring the kids. I still care about it, and I don’t want to sound like I don’t, because that matters to me. But I’ve stopped initiating as much. Reaching out, planning, showing up with that sense of purpose — it just feels harder and less rewarding than it used to.

Even keeping a clean calendar, which sounds boring, but for me that used to feel satisfying, like I had things in order. Now it’s more like this dull obligation sitting there.

So I can still recognize that these things matter to me, but the enjoyment or pull toward them is… muted. Flat. Heavy. I’m not completely unable to feel pleasure, but I’ve definitely stopped naturally reaching for the things that usually keep me connected to myself.

I still care about it, and I don’t want to sound like I don’t, because that matters to me. But I’ve stopped initiating as much. Now it’s more like this dull obligation sitting there.\n\nSo I can still recognize that these things matter to me, but the enjoyment or pull toward them is… muted. Flat. Heavy. I’m not completely unable to feel pleasure, but I’ve definitely stopped naturally reaching for the things that usually keep me connected to myself.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I know I kind of already touched on this, but that’s really the part that made me decide I needed to talk to someone.

It’s not that I can’t feel pleasure in anything at all, like I’m completely numb across the board. But the things that usually make me feel like myself have gotten really flat and heavy. Photography walks are a big one — normally I’d want to get out with my camera, notice little details, light, buildings, people, whatever. Lately I just don’t initiate it. I think about going and it already feels like too much effort, like there’s no pull there.

And mentoring the kids, too. That’s something I care about, but I’ve stopped being the one to reach out or plan things as much. Even keeping my calendar clean, which sounds small, but it usually gives me a sense of order and satisfaction… now it just feels like another weight.

So I don’t know if I’d say “almost all activities,” but the usual things that keep me connected to myself aren’t giving me much back right now. They feel muted, and I’ve stopped starting them the way I normally would.

Now I don’t initiate it much, and even when I think about reaching out, there’s this dullness to it. Same with my calendar and work organization — normally that gives me a sense of control or competence, but lately it just feels like pushing myself through motions. So, yeah, I can still do some of it if it’s in front of me, but the enjoyment and the spark are pretty muted.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad most of the day, exactly. That word feels… I’m not sure. It’s more like flat or weighed down, like I was saying. Sometimes I’ll catch myself thinking, “I should feel something about this,” but I’m not necessarily sitting there crying or feeling actively sad all day.\n\nThere are moments where I feel low, sure, but if you asked me to track it hour by hour, I don’t know that I could honestly say, yes, sad most of the day. It’s more muted than sad, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find pleasure in anything at all lately?\n\nPatient: A little, maybe, but it’s pretty muted. That’s part of what scared me enough to come in. The things that usually make me feel like myself — going out for photography walks, mentoring the kids, even keeping my work calendar really clean and organized — they’ve all started feeling flat and heavy.\n\nIt’s not like I hate them now. If I’m already doing something, I can sometimes notice that it should feel good, or that it used to feel good. Like with photography, I’ll still see a nice angle or the light hitting a building and think, “That’s interesting.” But it doesn’t really turn into pleasure. It’s more like an observation than enjoyment.\n\nAnd the bigger thing is I’m not starting things on my own. I used to naturally reach for the camera or check in about mentoring, and now there’s this heaviness before I even begin. So… pleasure isn’t completely gone, I guess, but it feels very dulled down, and I’m not seeking things out the way I normally would.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I haven’t been tracking it closely. My clothes don’t feel dramatically different or anything that I’ve noticed, and I haven’t been weighing myself regularly.\n\nI mean, maybe there are normal little fluctuations, but nothing where I’d say, “Oh, I’ve clearly lost weight,” or “I’ve gained a lot.” If you need an exact number, I’d probably have to check, because right now I’d just be guessing.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost any weight unintentionally?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve noticed, no. I mean, I haven’t stepped on a scale and tracked it in any careful way, so I don’t want to say absolutely not if I don’t actually know. But there hasn’t been anything obvious, like my pants suddenly being loose or people commenting on it or me thinking, “Wow, I’m thinner.”\n\nSo if there has been any change, it hasn’t been noticeable to me. I wouldn’t say I’ve unintentionally lost weight, at least not in a way I can point to.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still look forward to or feel excited about?\n\nPatient: Not really excited, no. I keep trying to think of something because I don’t want to sound like everything is completely gone, but… the things I normally would look forward to just don’t pull me in the same way.\n\nLike photography walks used to be a real thing for me — I’d notice the weather or the light and think, “I should take the camera out.” Or with mentoring the kids, I used to feel some purpose around that, like it mattered and I was glad to show up. Even something boring like keeping my work calendar clean and organized used to make me feel settled, like I was on top of things. And lately all of that feels flat and heavy.\n\nThe part that worries me is I’m not really initiating any of it. If something is already in front of me, I can sort of go through the motions, but I’m not looking forward to it ahead of time. I’m not getting that little spark of, “Oh, I want to do this.” That’s actually a big reason I came in — I stopped reaching for the things that usually make me feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still enjoy or find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Enjoy is probably too strong a word. I can still do some of the activities, but I don’t really get pleasure from them the way I used to.\n\nLike, with photography walks, I might think about it in this distant way, like, “That’s something I used to like,” but I don’t feel that pull to actually grab the camera and go out. Mentoring the kids is similar — I know it matters, and I know it used to make me feel useful and connected, but lately it feels more like I’m just showing up because I’m supposed to, not because I’m getting anything from it emotionally.\n\nEven something small like keeping my calendar clean — which sounds silly, but it used to give me this little sense of order, like I had things under control — now it just feels flat and heavy. That’s really what made me come in, honestly. The things that usually make me feel like myself stopped feeling rewarding, and then I noticed I wasn’t initiating them anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure from activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s fair to say. I don’t know if it’s completely unable, like I can still recognize that something is “supposed” to be enjoyable, if that makes sense. But the actual feeling of pleasure just isn’t really there.\n\nWhat made me come in is that the things that usually make me feel like myself — photography walks, mentoring the kids, even something as small as keeping a clean calendar — started feeling flat and heavy. And then I noticed I just stopped initiating them. I wasn’t thinking, “Oh, I want to go take pictures,” or “I’m looking forward to seeing the kids,” or even getting that little satisfying feeling from being organized. It’s more like I know those things used to matter to me, but I’m kind of standing outside of them now.\n\nSo yes, I guess I’d say I’m not really experiencing pleasure from them the way I used to. They’re still there, but the reward part feels muted or missing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things you've done or haven't done?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say “often,” exactly. But I’m not walking around with this constant, crushing guilt about something specific I did.\n\nIt’s more vague than that, if anything. Like I’ll think, “I should be doing better,” or “I’m letting things slide,” but I don’t know if that’s guilt or just frustration with myself. I don’t have one big thing I’m blaming myself for. It’s more… I notice I’m not doing what I normally do, and then I feel kind of disappointed in myself, but I’m not sure I’d call it guilt in the strong sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant weight loss over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve really noticed, no. I mean, my clothes don’t feel dramatically different or anything, and I haven’t had one of those moments where I step on a scale and think, “Oh wow, that’s a big drop.”\n\nI also haven’t been tracking it very closely, so I don’t want to sound overly certain. My weight can move around a little depending on routine and how busy I am, but as far as significant weight loss over the past few months… I don’t think so. Nothing that stood out to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving more slowly than usual or having trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah… more the trouble sitting still side, I think. Not so much that I’m physically pacing every second, but during those stretches I get this feeling like if I stop moving from one thing to the next, something important is going to fall through the cracks.\n\nI’ll start rebuilding work templates, then I’ll jump into making care plans, then I’m emailing relatives, then reorganizing files — and it’s all kind of happening at once. It doesn’t feel random to me in the moment; it feels like I’m being productive and responsible, like I’m finally catching everything. But looking at it after, it’s a lot. I’m bouncing between tasks, opening folders, making lists, revising systems that probably didn’t need revising right then.\n\nSo yes, sitting still can feel uncomfortable, but not just in a restless-leg kind of way. It’s more like sitting still means I’m dropping the ball, or being careless, and I need to keep doing something to stay ahead of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving more slowly than you used to?\n\nPatient: Not really, no.
